Title: Faithful Federated Tabular Data Synthesis

URL Source: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.33403v1

Published Time: Tue, 27 Oct 2026 00:26:57 GMT

Number of Pages: 74

Markdown Content:
# Faithful Federated Tabular Data Synthesis 

Zilong Cao ∗1 , Hai Zhang †1 , and Xuan Bi ‡2 1School of Mathematics, Northwest University 

> 2

Carlson School of Management, University of Minnesota 

Abstract 

Synthetic data is an important data source in the AI era. This paper studies the generation of mixed-type tabular synthetic data in federated settings, where two key challenges arise: ensuring distributional fidelity and effectively handling mixed-type data. We propose Federated Integrative Two-Step Synthesis (FITS), a framework for mixed-type tabular data synthesis. At its core is a two-step federated algorithm for estimating conditional cumulative distribution functions (CDFs) using local linear regression. Building on this algorithm, we develop a federated synthesis procedure based on the probability chain rule to ensure distributional fidelity. To handle mixed-type data, FITS introduces a continualization method that lifts discrete variables into a continuous space and recovers their discrete distributions without loss at both the density-function and CDF levels. This transformation provides a unified continuous representation of mixed-type variables, facilitating the application of local linear regression and synthetic data generation. Our theoretical analysis characterizes the conditional density and cumulative distribution functions of the continualized mixed-type variables and establishes the uniform convergence of the resulting federated conditional CDF estimators. Experiments demonstrate that FITS-generated synthetic data improves downstream task performance for all clients, with the benefits depending on how the synthetic data is used. 

1 Introduction 

Synthetic data generation has received increasing attention as the AI era progresses, with synthetic data becoming an important data source in many fields, such as large language models [Wang et al., 2023], imaging [Kim et al., 2022], and medical research [Kokosi and Harron, 2022]. However, synthetic data generation (or, broadly speaking, data synthesis) is itself a nontrivial methodological problem, rather than a simple consequence of assuming a generative distribution [Alaa et al., 2022]. In addition to the intrinsic challenges of generating synthetic data, a fundamental issue is the limited availability of real data, which hinders the production of high-quality synthetic data. Such scarcity frequently arises from data silos, where data holders prioritize privacy and legal considerations. This severely limits or even prevents access to their data. Federated learning (FL, McMahan et al. [2016], Kairouz and McMahan [2021]) provides an efficient and privacy-preserving framework for synthetic data generation, addressing this dilemma through a distributed paradigm that eliminates centralized    

> ∗Email: nwu_czl@stumail.nwu.edu.cn
> †Email: zhanghai@nwu.edu.cn
> ‡Corresponding author. Email: xbi@umn.edu

1arXiv:2610.33403v1 [cs.LG] 24 Oct 2026 storage and avoids centralizing raw data while transmitting only processed data from clients to ensure security. Numerous federated synthetic data generation efforts focus on images and other data formats [Little et al., 2023]; nonetheless, the development of synthetic tabular data generation in federated learning remains limited, despite its substantial importance across various domains. Existing studies have explored generative adversarial networks [Zhao et al., 2021], diffusion models [Sattarov and Schreyer, 2025], and marginal-based synthesis [Li et al., 2026]. However, several major challenges remain unresolved. (i) Communication costs remain substantial. Federated synthetic data generation methods, especially deep learning-based models, require extensive communication to learn the generative model. Several reports [Zhu et al., 2019, Kairouz and McMahan, 2021, National Institute of Standards and Technology, 2025] emphasize that reducing communication is essential for improving both the efficiency and safety of federated learning. (ii) Lack of faithfulness remains a concern [Alaa et al., 2022]. Generative adversarial networks and diffusion models lack theoretical interpretability, which may lead to synthetic data that is unfaithful. For example, many deep-learning-based models cannot guarantee that the synthetic data is faithful to the original distribution. Marginal-based synthesis, on the other hand, relies only on low-order marginal information and may have limitations in approximating high-dimensional distributions. (iii) Existing methods remain inefficient in handling mixed-type data. For example, black-box approaches, such as generative adversarial networks and diffusion models, typically use one-hot encodings or continuous embeddings for discrete variables; however, one-hot encodings are not genuinely continuous and substantially increase dimensionality, whereas non-invertible continuous embeddings destroy the discrete structure and compromise interpretability. Marginal-based synthesis, by contrast, discretizes continuous variables to facilitate the computation of marginal statistics, thereby losing information carried by continuous variables. Nonparametric methods provide established approaches to handling mixed-type data. Li and Racine [2008] estimate conditional CDFs and quantiles using generalized product kernels that combine continuous and discrete kernels. Alternatively, Nagler [2018] shows that suitably chosen independent noisy jitter allows nonparametric density estimators designed for continuous data to be applied to mixed data. These approaches provide important foundations for mixed-data estimation but do not develop a federated synthesis procedure. To address these challenges, we propose a federated framework, termed federated integrative two-step synthesis for mixed-type tabular data (FITS), which supports the generation of multivariate mixed-type tabular data based on the chain rule. FITS is characterized by a two-step communication procedure. In the first step, clients transmit weighted Hessian-type matrices to the central server, which computes the inverse of the aggregated matrix and sends only its first row back to the clients. In the second step, clients send their local response-smoothed CDF contribution vectors to the central server. The server then constructs the global conditional cumulative distribution functions (CDFs), which are subsequently used for inverse sampling and data synthesis. For mixed-type data, we employ an efficient technique that lifts discrete variables into a continuous space while allowing inversion back to the original discrete space without incurring additional dimensional cost. FITS includes a core federated algorithm for estimating conditional cumulative distribution functions via local linear regression. In theory, local linear regression (LLR, Fan et al. [1996]) can effectively reduce estimator bias and has been studied for conditional CDF estimation [Hall et al., 1999, Xie, 2023] and widely used in various applications [Cai et al., 2011, Cheng and Wu, 2013, Chakrabortty and Cai, 2017, Cheng et al., 2020]. Furthermore, we propose client-designed and penalty-adaptive kernels to enable effective LLR fitting in federated settings with mixed-type data. At the client level, the client-designed kernel offers both fixed-bandwidth and K-nearest-neighbor (KNN) variants, with 2the choice determined by each client. Under this design, each client can specify kernel components, including the kernel function and bandwidth parameters. The penalty-adaptive kernel, by contrast, operates at the kernel-function level and penalizes continualized samples whose categories do not match that of the query. FITS offers several distinctive advantages for federated synthesis of mixed-type tabular data. First, unlike iterative federated optimization, FITS requires only the upload of processed data and a two-step procedure to achieve distribution estimation. Second, by estimating conditional CDFs and combining them through the chain rule, FITS directly targets the underlying joint distribution, providing a principled basis for distributionally faithful data synthesis. Third, we utilize an invertible continualization technique that maps continuous and discrete variables into a unified continuous domain without increasing the data dimension. This transformation preserves the recoverability of the original discrete variables while providing a foundation for applying established methods developed for continuous data and facilitates synthetic data generation through a unified procedure. Finally, FITS supports client-designed kernels that accommodate client-specific data characteristics and a penalty-adaptive mechanism that reduces kernel-fitting error caused by category mismatches after continualization. Our contributions can be summarized as follows: From a methodological perspective, we propose a novel FITS framework for federated mixed-type tabular data synthesis. It supports federated conditional CDF estimation and synthetic data generation. In addition, FITS introduces an invertible discrete-to-continuous lifting technique, the client-designed kernel for the FL scheme, and the penalty-adaptive kernel for the mixed-type data. In theory, we characterize the proposed continualization technique at both the density and distribution-function levels, showing, in particular, its blockwise smoothness. Moreover, under this blockwise structure, we establish uniform convergence rates for the federated local linear conditional CDF estimator using Gaussian kernels with unbounded support. In particular, direct kernel smoothing near blockwise boundaries can yield inconsistent estimates; we therefore develop a modified estimation procedure that restores consistency at these points. Finally, we establish the uniform consistency of the per-step conditional CDF estimates used in the chain-rule generation process. We evaluate FITS on simulated and real datasets under imbalanced client sample sizes and compare it with two federated data synthesis baselines, HeteroFedSyn [Li et al., 2026] and FedTabDiff [Sattarov and Schreyer, 2025], using three downstream models: linear regression, logistic regression, and FT-Transformer (a deep learning model, [Gorishniy et al., 2021]), covering both regression and classification tasks. The results show that FITS-generated synthetic data can substantially improve downstream performance and provide the best distributional fidelity compared to synthetic data generated by the other methods. The remainder of the paper is organized as follows. Section 2 introduces the federated setup, notation, and local linear formulation of conditional CDF estimation. Section 3 presents FITS for continuous data, including two-step federated aggregation, client-designed kernel schemes, and synthetic data generation through the chain rule and inverse sampling. Section 4 extends the framework to mixed-type data through continualization of discrete variables and penalty-adaptive kernels. Section 5 establishes uniform convergence results for both continuous and continualized mixed-type data. Section 6 evaluates the utility of synthetic data through simulation and real-data studies. The final section summarizes the findings and outlines future research directions. The appendices provide auxiliary theoretical tools, detailed proofs, and supplementary experiments. 32 Background 

Subsection 2.1 establishes the federated learning setup and the general notation used throughout the paper. Subsection 2.2 introduces local linear regression, explains how it estimates conditional CDFs, and describes its adaptation to the federated learning scheme.

F (z | x) = P(Z ≤ z | X = x), z ∈ Z , x ∈ X . (1) It is the canonical object from which arbitrary conditional functionals, including means, quantiles, and probability masses, can be derived, and samples can be generated by inverse-CDF sampling. The federated system is subject to the following communication and data-access constraints. The clients communicate with the CS in synchronous rounds; in each round, a client may upload one or more deterministic functions of its local data Hk (and any quantities previously received from the CS), and the CS may broadcast a deterministic function of the received uploads to all clients. The following raw-data objects are never transmitted: the individual samples {h(k) 

> i

}i, the local response vectors {h(k)

> i,d +1

}i, and the local design matrices used in weighted regression. The federated estimator developed in Section 3 respects these constraints by transmitting only aggregated sufficient statistics —a (d+1) × (d+1) design summary in the first round and an L-dimensional CDF contribution vector in the second round—each of which is a many-to-one function of the underlying raw data. 

Coordinate grids. For each coordinate j ∈ [d] we fix an evaluation grid gj = ( g1j , . . . , g Gj )⊤ ∈ RG,and write G = {gj }d+1

. The grids serve two purposes: in Section 3, they parameterize the evaluation points of bF (· | x). And the grids are used to generate synthetic data via linear interpolation.

3 The Proposed Method 

This section develops our framework FITS for continuous data, focusing on federated conditional CDF estimation and synthetic data generation. Subsection 3.1 formulates the two-step FL procedure for estimating LLR-based conditional CDFs, develops the core algorithm, and analyzes its communication cost and equivalence to the pooled setting. Subsection 3.2 presents two interchangeable bandwidth schemes (fixed-bandwidth and KNN-kernel). Subsection 3.3 proposes a method for federated synthetic data generation that combines the chain rule with inverse sampling from conditional CDFs. 

3.1 Two-step federated aggregation 

We describe our two-step federated aggregation procedure first, whose purpose is to perform federated estimation of the closed-form expression in (4). The first step calculates the clients’ kernel-weighted Hessian matrices, and the CS aggregates them and broadcasts the first-row vector of the inverse of 5Figure 1: FITS: two-step aggregation. the aggregated Hessian matrix. In the second step, clients upload their vectors of conditional CDF estimates, after which the CS aggregates and post-processes them to obtain the final conditional CDF estimation vector. Figure 1 illustrates the two-step procedure.

Remark 3.1 (Communication cost) . The two-step procedure requires, per query x: (i) Round 1. Each client uploads a symmetric (d+1) × (d+1) matrix, costing (d+1)( d+2) /2 scalars per client; the CS broadcasts a (d+1) -dimensional vector q(x). (ii) Round 2. Each client uploads an L-dimensional 

7CDF vector with elements bF (k)(zi | x), i ∈ [L]. The per-query communication is therefore O(d2 + L)

per client, independent of the local sample size mk.

The full procedure is given in Algorithm 1 and the communication cost is O(d2 + L) as stated in Remark 3.1. Here, in Algorithm 1, Wk(x; Hk, r 0) denotes the client-designed procedure described in Subsection 3.2, where clients can choose their own kernel-fitting schemes. It returns the selected local samples together with their weights ϖ, and allows each client to adaptively choose a kernel-fitting scheme, either the fixed-bandwidth scheme or the KNN-kernel scheme. The fixed-bandwidth scheme uses all local hold-out samples, so that N (k) 

> ck

= [ mk]. By contrast, the KNN-kernel scheme searches for the neighbors of x and uses a subset N (k) 

> ck

⊂ [mk].Combining (6)–(8) with (4) yields the following algebraic equivalence between the federated and pooled estimators.

Proposition 3.1 establishes that Algorithm 1 is not an approximation of the centralized estimator but an exact reformulation of it under federated data access. The practical implication is that all statistical properties derived for the centralized estimator—in particular the uniform convergence rates of Section 5—transfer to the federated estimator without further argument. 

3.2 Client-designed kernel 

In this subsection, we propose two client-designed kernel schemes: the fixed-bandwidth scheme and the KNN-kernel scheme, supporting the Wk(x; Hk, r 0) procedure in line 3 of Algorithm 1. Clients may choose either scheme to select local samples and compute their kernel weights. Moreover, both schemes leverage the client’s local data to standardize the selected samples and the query point before kernel fitting, thereby improving the stability and effectiveness of kernel fitting. The standardization procedure is performed entirely on the client side: the marginal means, standard deviations, and raw covariate scales are not transmitted, in accordance with the data-access constraints in Subsection 2.1. 

Fixed-bandwidth scheme. Under the fixed-bandwidth scheme, each client uses all of its local hold-out samples, so that N (k) 

> ck

= [ mk], together with a client-specific covariate bandwidth w(k) 

> x

> 0.After the covariate coordinates have been locally standardized using the client’s marginal means and standard deviations, the weight assigned to observation i is

Similarly, the effective sample size problem also affects the pooled method. A possible approach is based on KNN-type methods [Fan and Gijbels, 1992, Benezet et al., 2025]. 

KNN-kernel scheme. Under the KNN-kernel scheme, the effective bandwidth is chosen adaptively at each client based on the distance to the ck-th nearest neighbor of the query point. The local KNN budgets are allocated in proportion to the hold-out sample sizes:

Remark 3.2. A separate design choice concerns the scale on which the local linear expansion is formed. The kernel weights ϖ(k) 

> i

are evaluated in the locally standardized covariate space, but the local linear design vector U (k) 

> i

= ι(h(k) 

> i, :d

− x) is constructed in the original coordinate space. This mismatch is intentional. Standardization stabilizes the kernel-fitting step under heterogeneous client scales; keeping the design in the native scale ensures that the local linear expansion targets F (z | x) in the original covariate system, so that the federated estimator remains an estimator of the population conditional distribution on X rather than on a client-dependent rescaling thereof.

3.3 Federated Synthetic Data Generation 

We combine the chain rule with inverse sampling from the federated conditional CDF estimator 

bF (z | x) to generate synthetic data. Without loss of generality, we assume that the chain order follows the variable sequence from X1 to Xd+1 and denote X:j = ( X1, . . . , Xj ). The j-th sampling step is based on the one-dimensional conditional distribution function given the previously sampled 

j − 1 dimensions: 

ˆxj = bF −1(U | ˆx:j−1 = (ˆ x1, . . . , ˆxj−1)) , (15) where U ∼ Unif[0 , 1] , ˆxi denotes the sampled value of the i-th coordinate, and bF −1(· | ˆx:j−1) denotes the estimated inverse conditional CDF given the event ˆx:j−1 = (ˆ x1, . . . , ˆxj−1). For notational convenience, we denote bF (z | ˆx:0 ) as the unconditional CDF, which is equivalent to the CDF estimate for the first variable bF (z). For conditional CDF estimation, we estimate the conditional CDF using Algorithm 1 and invert the estimate using linear interpolation. For the first dimension, we use the following smooth KDE-based CDF estimator:

Values between adjacent grid points are obtained by linear interpolation. Specifically, if U ∈ ( bF (zℓ−1 | ˆx:j−1), bF (zℓ |

ˆx:j−1)) , then 

ˆxj = zℓ−1 + U − bF (zℓ−1 | ˆx:j−1)

bF (zℓ | ˆx:j−1) − bF (zℓ−1 | ˆx:j−1) (zℓ − zℓ−1), ℓ ∈ [L]. (17) 

Remark 3.3 (Cost of synthetic data generation) . For D variables, B synthetic records, K clients, and L response grid points, each conditional CDF query with r conditioning variables requires each client to upload (r + 1)( r + 2) /2 Hessian entries and L CDF values. Thus, direct execution of the conditional generation steps in Algorithm 2 requires O KB (D3 + DL ) uploaded scalars in total. 

4 Generalization to Mixed-Type Data 

In this section, we propose a continualization method for discrete variables. In Subsection 4.1, the proposed continualization procedure lifts discrete variables into a continuous space and allows exact inversion back to the original discrete space without information loss. This method effectively mitigates the impact of mismatches between continualized discrete samples used for kernel fitting and the query point. 

4.1 Continualization of discrete variables 

The lifting is constructed independently for each discrete coordinate; hence, this subsection focuses on the univariate case. Discrete variables include ordered variables and unordered (nominal/categorical) variables. For ordered variables, we lift the discrete support into continuous adjacent intervals. For unordered variables, we fix an arbitrary one-to-one numerical coding to define the continualization intervals. 

Ordered discrete variables. Let Υ be a discrete random variable with finite ordered support 

Υ = {υ1, . . . , υ L}, υ1 < · · · < υ L, pℓ = P(Υ = υℓ),

Unordered (nominal) discrete variables. For an unordered categorical variable with support 

{c1, . . . , c L}, the same construction applies after fixing an arbitrary one-to-one coding cℓ ↔ ℓ. We set

The coding is used to define the partition and inverse map and introduces a Euclidean metric. We summarize the properties of the univariate construction here. The more general multidimensional construction is presented in Subsection 4.2, together with the corresponding theoretical results, Theorem 5.2 and Corollary 5.2. 

Proposition 4.1 (Univariate continualization) . Let Υ be a discrete variable on Υ = {υ1, . . . , υ L}

(ordered or nominal) and let Y be its continualization as in (18) . Then: (i) Mass preservation. P(Y ∈ Iℓ) = pℓ = P(Υ = υℓ) for every ℓ ∈ [L].(ii) Exact inverse. For ordered variables, ceil( Y ) = Υ almost surely; for nominal variables, 

cat( Y ) = Υ almost surely. (iii) Piecewise regularity. The density fY is piecewise constant on the partition {Iℓ}ℓ, and the distribution function FY is continuous on S 

> ℓ

Iℓ and piecewise affine within each Iℓ.

Properties (i)–(iii) follow directly from the definition and therefore require no further proof.

The block I(υ) also defines a corresponding volume |I(υ)|, which is used to normalize the lifted density function. Let Y denote the joint continualization of X(d), obtained by applying (18) coordinatewise with mutually independent uniform random variables. The resulting continualized vector is 

eX =  X(c), Y ∈ X c × Y =: eX . (22) Throughout the paper, x denotes a generic point in the original mixed-type covariate space X , while 

˜x = ( x(c), y) denotes the corresponding point in eX . The inverse discrete map 

˜x 7 →  x(c), cat 1(y1), . . . , cat ˜d(y ˜d)

recovers the original mixed-type variable exactly. The block structure naturally separates eX into an interior region and a boundary region. Define 

eX ◦ := Xc × Y ◦ = Xc × [

> υ∈Υ

I(υ)◦, (23) where I(υ)◦ denotes the topological interior of I(υ) and Y◦ denotes the union of the interiors of all blocks. Its complement is eX • := Xc × Y •, Y• := Y \ Y ◦.

This set is the union of all block boundaries in the y-coordinates and will be referred to as the covariate jumping set (block-boundary set), since the lifted joint density of eX may exhibit jump discontinuities across eX •.This construction yields a piecewise smooth lifted law. On each block Xc × I(υ)◦, the joint density of eX inherits the smoothness of the conditional density of X(c) given X(d) = υ, because the continualization is uniform within the corresponding discrete cell. Across eX •, the density may jump, since the conditional densities of X(c) on adjacent blocks need not match. Thus the lifted law is generally piecewise smooth rather than globally smooth. A formal piecewise-Cr regularity condition is given in Section 5, where it supports the blockwise uniform convergence analysis of conditional CDF estimation. The same continualization device is applied to the response when Z is discrete. Specifically, if Z ∈ { z1, . . . , z L} with z1 < · · · < z L, set z0 := z1 − 1, Ii = ( zi−1, z i], and define ˜Z = Z − V ,where V ∼ Unif(0 , |IZ |) is independent of (Z, eX). Then ˜Z ∈ eZ := SLi=1 Ii, and the target in (1) is estimated as F (˜ z | ˜x). If Z is already continuous, no response continualization is performed and we set ˜z = z. (X1, X 2); on (−1, 0] and (0 , 1] , it is affine in y, with forms pϕ0(x1)(1+ y) and pϕ0(x1)+(1 −p)ϕ1(x1)y,respectively. 14 To fix ideas, consider a bivariate covariate x = ( x1, x 2), where x1 is continuous on a compact set 

X1 ⊂ R and x2 ∈ { 0, 1} is binary with P(x2 = 0) = p. Suppose that the joint mass-density f (x1, x 2)

is continuous in x1 for each value of x2 and the marginal density f (x1) > 0 is continuous on X1.Then 

f (x1, x 2) = f (x1, 0) I{x2 = 0 } + f (x1, 1) I{x2 = 1 }.

Under binary continualization, let Y = X2 − U , where U ∼ Unif(0 , 1) is independent of X2. The continualized variable takes values in (−1, 0] ∪ (0 , 1] , with blocks I(0) = ( −1, 0] and I(1) = (0 , 1] ,and the lifted covariate is ˜x = ( x1, y ) ∈ X 1 × (−1, 1] . The lifted joint density has the blockwise form 

g(x1, y ) = f (x1, 0) I{y ∈ I(0) } + f (x1, 1) I{y ∈ I(1) }.

Thus g is continuous, and as smooth as f (·, j ), within each of the two open blocks X1 ×(−1, 0) and 

X1 ×(0 , 1) , but may jump across the internal interface X1 ×{ 0} whenever f (x1, 0) ̸ = f (x1, 1) . Figure 3 illustrates this lifted density and the corresponding change from a stepwise discrete-coordinate CDF to a piecewise affine continualized CDF. The same blockwise interpretation applies to conditional quantities. For example, on the block corresponding to x2 = 0 ,

g(x1 | y) = f (x1 | 0) = f (x1, 0) 

p , y ∈ (−1, 0] ,

and 

g(y | x1) = P(X2 = 0 | x1) = f (x1, 0) 

f (x1) , y ∈ (−1, 0] .

These lifted conditional functions are continuous in x1 within the block and constant in the continualized coordinate y. The block X1 × (0 , 1] is treated analogously, with p replaced by 1 − p and 

f (x1, 0) replaced by f (x1, 1) . Finally, applying the inverse categorization map, here the ceiling map in (19), collapses X1 × (−1, 0] and X1 × (0 , 1] back to X1 × { 0} and X1 × { 1}, respectively. Hence continualization creates a blockwise continuous representation without losing the original mixed-type information. 

4.4 Penalty-adaptive kernel 

The continualization in Section 4.1 maps each discrete value υℓ to the interval Iℓ = ( υℓ−1, υ ℓ], and the continualized query value yq lies somewhere inside the interval associated with its category. If 

yq, a continualized coordinate of the query point, is close to an interval boundary, a Euclidean KNN search in the continualized space may select observations that are geometrically close but belong to different discrete categories. For the fixed-bandwidth scheme, kernel evaluation at yq may also assign excessive weight to mismatched neighboring observations, thereby affecting the effective fitting weights of observations with matched categories. Such neighbors are undesirable because locality should respect both the continualized geometry and the recovered discrete labels. We address this boundary effect by replacing each discrete covariate coordinate of the query point with the center of its continualization interval before standardization, neighbor selection, and kernel evaluation: 

y⋄ 

> q

← a⋄   

> q, cat q(yq)

:= midpoint of Iq, cat q (yq ), q ∈ D , (24) where D is the index set of discrete variables. Because the lifted law is uniform within each discrete block (Proposition 4.1 and Theorem 5.3), this query-side substitution does not alter the target 15 Figure 4: Boundary mismatches. conditional law within the corresponding block. The corresponding theoretical guarantee is provided in Corollary 5.3. Its role is purely geometric: it moves the query point away from block interfaces so that the selected neighborhood is consistent with the query’s discrete label. We further multiply the continuous kernel weight by a category-aware penalty. Define

λq > 0, preserving the ordering information of the original support. For nominal variables, a category mismatch receives a multiplicative discount γq ∈ (0 , 1] , independent of the numerical coding of the labels. Thus, the mixed-type adaptation consists of two local deterministic modifications of the weighting procedure: a query-side interval-center substitution and a multiplicative category penalty. Together, they make the final kernel weight reflect both geometric proximity in the continualized space and categorical proximity in the recovered discrete label space. No changes are made to the two-step aggregation logic or to the standardization-versus-design scale separation described above. In particular, the algebraic identity in Proposition 3.1 continues to hold for the mixed-type estimator, provided that the centralized local linear estimator is defined using the same weighting modifications. 

5 Theoretical Analysis 

In this section, we develop uniform convergence results for the federated local linear conditional CDF estimator introduced in Section 3. The convergence is uniform not only over the covariates but also over the response variable. Specifically, the uniformity over the response applies to the function estimator ˆF (z | x) for all z ∈ R and provides a theoretical guarantee for the quality of 16 data generation based on ˆF (z | x). By Proposition 3.1, the federated estimator coincides exactly with the centralized local linear estimator computed on the pooled sample whenever the kernel weights are matched. The convergence analysis can therefore be carried out for the centralized estimator with total sample size M = PKk=1 mk, and the resulting rates transfer directly to the federated estimator. Throughout this section, we consider the asymptotic regime M → ∞ and let the bandwidths w(k) 

> x

, w (k) 

> z

→ 0 at rates to be specified. Subsection 5.1 states the regularity assumptions on the covariate marginal density, the conditional distribution function, and the kernels. Subsection 5.2 establishes uniform convergence in the basic setting where all covariates and the response are continuous, and serves as the analytical backbone for the mixed-variable case. Subsection 5.3 characterizes the piecewise smoothness of the lifted joint and conditional distributions induced by continualization, providing the regularity needed for the mixed-variable analysis. Subsection 5.4 then extends the uniform convergence results to the continualized mixed-type setting, both on interior blocks and on the covariate jumping set, and establishes a strictly sharper rate for the conditional probability mass estimator when the response is discrete. 

5.1 Assumptions 

We work with three groups of assumptions: a geometric and smoothness condition on the covariate marginal density, a smoothness condition on the conditional distribution function, and a regularity condition on the kernels. The first two assumptions follow the standard local linear regression setup [Xie, 2023] and are stated on a generic covariate domain X ⊂ Rd; the same conditions will be invoked blockwise in Subsection 5.4. The kernel assumption is generalized to the Gaussian kernel with unbounded support. 

Assumption 1 (Marginal density) . The covariate domain X ⊂ Rd is compact and convex, and satisfies an internal cone condition: there exist constants λ0, λ 1 ∈ (0 , 1] such that, for every x ∈ X 

and every w ∈ (0 , λ 0], there exists x′ ∈ X with B(x′, λ 1w) ⊂ B(x, w ) ∩ X , where B(x, δ ) denotes the Euclidean ball centered at x with radius δ. The covariate marginal density f satisfies: (i) inf x∈X f (x) > 0;(ii) f ∈ C2(X ◦), with all second-order partial derivatives uniformly continuous and bounded on the interior domain X ◦.

Assumption 2 (Conditional distribution) . The conditional distribution function F (z | x) = P(Z ≤

z | X = x) is twice continuously differentiable on S = Z ×X , where Z ⊆ R, and all partial derivatives of order up to two are bounded and uniformly continuous on S.

Assumption 3 (Kernel functions) . The kernel functions used to define the weights ϖi = κ(·) with the fixed bandwidth wx used in Section 3 are taken to be Gaussian: κ(u) = ϕ(u) := (2 π)−1/2e−u2/2,

K(u) = Φ(u) := R u 

> −∞

ϕ(v) dv , and κ(t) = Qdj=1 ϕ(tj ).

Assumption 1 is the standard interior cone condition under which uniform kernel approximation arguments apply. Assumption 2 is the second-order smoothness condition required for local linear bias control. Assumption 3 generalizes the kernel family from kernels with bounded support to kernels with unbounded support, specifically the Gaussian kernel. 17 We adopt a common-bandwidth regime across clients in federated learning. All clients use the same covariate bandwidth w(k) 

> x

= wx > 0 and the same response bandwidth w(k) 

> z

= wz > 0. They satisfy wx, w z → 0 and M w dx/| log wx| → ∞ as M → ∞ . This is the standard fixed-bandwidth regime for d-dimensional local linear smoothing. 

5.2 Uniform convergence under continuous covariates 

To isolate the analytic core, we first consider the case where all covariates are continuous. The federated estimator bF (z | x) at the query point (x, z ) ∈ X × R equals the centralized local linear intercept (4) on the pooled sample, and so its analysis reduces to the standard local linear conditional CDF analysis. We state the result here in a form that will be reused blockwise in Subsection 5.4.

When at least one covariate coordinate is discrete and is handled through the continualization of Section 4.1, neither Assumption 1 nor Assumption 2 holds globally on eX : the lifted density generally jumps across eX •, and the lifted conditional distribution function inherits this jump structure. The remainder of this section adapts Theorem 5.1 to the lifted setting by exploiting the blockwise regularity of the lifted laws. 18 5.3 Regularity of the lifted joint and conditional laws 

The lifted covariate vector eX = ( X(c), Y) defined in Section 4.2 has a joint density on eX = Xc × Y 

that is piecewise smooth rather than globally smooth: on each open block Xc × I(υ)◦ it inherits the regularity of the conditional law of X(c) given X(d) = υ, while across the covariate jumping set eX •

it may exhibit finite jumps. This subsection makes that clear and then derives the corresponding piecewise structure of the lifted conditional distribution function, which is the regularity input required for the uniform convergence analysis in Subsection 5.4. 

5.3.1 Lifted joint density: blockwise continuity and one-sided limits 

We first describe the lifted joint density g at the level of C0-regularity, before turning to higher-order smoothness. 

Theorem 5.2 (Continuity of the lifted joint density) . Suppose that for every υ ∈ Υ the mixed density-mass section x(c) 7 → f (x(c), υ) is continuous on Xc. Let g denote the joint density of the continualized variable vector eX. Then the following hold. (i) Blockwise representation. For every υ ∈ Υ and every (x(c), y) ∈ X c × I(υ)◦,

g(x(c), y) = f (x(c), υ)

|I(υ)| ,

so g is continuous on each open block Xc × I(υ)◦ and independent of y on that block. (ii) Jumps across eX •. Let (x(c)0 , y0) ∈ eX •, and let υ, υ′ ∈ Υ be two indices of two blocks sharing the boundary on which y0 lies. Then the one-sided limits of g at (x(c)0 , y0) from inside Xc × I(υ)

and from inside Xc × I(υ′) exist and equal f (x(c)0 , υ)/|I(υ)| and f (x(c)0 , υ′)/|I(υ′)|, respectively; these limits coincide if and only if f (x(c)0 , υ)/|I(υ)| = f (x(c)0 , υ′)/|I(υ′)|.(iii) Piecewise continuity. The function g is piecewise continuous on eX ◦, and at every interface point (x(c)1 , y1) ∈ eX •, g|Xc×I(υ) admits a continuous extension to the closed block Xc × I(υ),given by the same formula in (i). 

The proof is given in Appendix C.2. Theorem 5.2 captures the basic geometric picture: continu-alization unfolds the discrete mass on Υ into a continuous density on eX that is constant in y within each block, has well-defined one-sided limits at every block boundary, and exhibits a finite jump exactly when the adjacent blockwise density values f (x(c), υ)/|I(υ)| differ at the corresponding x(c).Raising the smoothness assumption on the sections f (·, υ) to Cr extends this C0 picture to Cr in a directly analogous manner. The proof, given in Appendix C.3, applies Theorem 5.2 to each partial derivative of order at most r.

Corollary 5.2 (Piecewise Cr regularity of the lifted density) . Let r ≥ 0 be an integer and assume that for every υ ∈ Υ the section x(c) 7 → f (x(c), υ) belongs to Cr(Xc), with all partial derivatives of order up to r continuous on Xc uniformly in υ. Then: (i) For every υ ∈ Υ , g ∈ Cr Xc × I(υ)◦, with all y-derivatives identically zero and all x(c)-derivatives inherited from f (·, υ)/|I(υ)|.

19 (ii) Across eX •, the one-sided limits of g and of its x(c)-derivatives from adjacent blocks need not coincide; the magnitude of any such discontinuity follows the formula of Theorem 5.2(ii) applied to the corresponding derivative. (iii) The collection {g|Xc×I(υ)}υ∈Υ constitutes a piecewise Cr structure on eX . The restriction 

g|Xc×I(υ) admits a Cr extension to Xc × I(υ).

5.3.2 Lifted conditional distribution: blockwise C2 structure 

With the regularity of the joint density in hand, we now turn to the lifted conditional distribution function F (z | ˜x) = P(Z ≤ z | eX = ˜ x). Two features of this function are critical for the analysis of Subsection 5.4: it is independent of y within each block (so y-derivatives contribute no bias to local linear smoothing), and, in the case of an ordered discrete response, it is exactly affine in the continualized response coordinate ˜z on each response block (so the ˜z-direction Taylor remainder vanishes whenever the response smoothing window stays within a single block). 

Theorem 5.3 (Lifted conditional distribution) . Let eX = ( X(c), Y) be the continualized covariate random vector. (i) Continuous response. Suppose Z is continuous and, for every υ ∈ Υ , the section (z, x(c)) 7 →

F (z | x(c), υ) belongs to C2(Z × X c) with second-order partial derivatives uniformly continuous and bounded on Z × X c, uniformly in υ. Then 

F (z | x(c), y) = X

5.4 Uniform convergence under continualized mixed-type variates 

We now establish the uniform convergence rate of the federated local linear conditional CDF estimator under continualized mixed-type variates. The strategy is to apply Theorem 5.1 blockwise on each piecewise C2 region identified in Subsection 5.3, and then to combine the blockwise bounds using the finiteness of Υ . However, points near block boundaries require special care.

6 Experiments 

We compare FITS with two baseline methods, the marginal-based HeteroFedSyn [Li et al., 2026] and the diffusion model FedTabDiff [Sattarov et al., 2024]. We evaluate the synthetic data generators with three models, including linear regression and logistic regression for parameter estimation, and a deep learning model (FT-Transformer, [Gorishniy et al., 2021]) for prediction. The simulation study uses a controlled mixed-type data-generating mechanism with known downstream parameters, allowing direct assessment of parameter recovery under imbalanced federated sample sizes. The real-data study uses two mixed-type tabular data sets, one for regression and one for binary classification, to examine these generators’ utility in practice. Throughout this section, we ask not only whether synthetic data should replace local data, but also whether synthetic samples generated by FITS provide a statistically useful complement to client-level local data, especially for clients with limited sample sizes. This distinction is important: in federated settings, the value of synthetic data depends not only on the quality of the generator but also on how synthetic and local data is combined in the downstream tasks. Table 1 summarizes the datasets and experimental designs. 23 Table 1: Experimental designs for the simulation and real-data studies. > Test sample size 4,000 3,476 9,045

> Number of repetitions 50
> Clients 10

6.1 Experimental setup 

6.1.1 Federated split with sample-size imbalance 

All experiments use K = 10 clients. Unless otherwise stated, the training data is randomly per-muted and split according to the fixed proportion vector (p1, . . . , p 10 ) = (30% , 20% , 10% , 9% , 8% , 7% 

, 6% , 5% , 4% , 1%) . Thus client k receives approximately pkntrain observations. This creates a delib-erately imbalanced federated system: the largest client contains about 30% of the training data, whereas the smallest client contains about 1% .This design is intended to stress-test the downstream data usage modes under client-size imbalance. If all clients had comparable sample sizes, the relative advantage of synthetic data would be harder to separate from ordinary sampling variability. In contrast, under the present split, estimators from the local datasets only are expected to be high-variance on the smallest clients and comparatively stable on the largest clients. This makes it possible to assess whether synthetic data primarily helps small clients and whether it degrades performance on large clients. The main experiments focus on sample-size imbalance while keeping the federated split otherwise random. 

6.1.2 Federated generator configurations 

For FITS, we use the Gaussian-kernel scheme and the KNN-kernel scheme, abbreviated as FITS-G and FITS-K respectively. The general settings include that the target bandwidth parameter wz is set to 0.05 and the conditional CDF grid size is G = 300 . For covariates’ kernel-fitting, FITS-G takes the Gaussian kernel with Scott’s rule kernel bandwidth w(k) 

> x

= n−1/(d+4)  

> train

, where ntrain is the size of the full training dataset and the dimension d increases as the covariates are added along the chain process. FITS-K uses the Epanechnikov kernel with η = 1 .05 and the global KNN budget is dimension-adaptive: when dcond = 1 , r0 = 350 ; when dcond = 2 , r0 = 250 and otherwise, r0 = 200 .For HeteroFedSyn and FedTabDiff, Appendix E gives the detailed configurations. 24 6.1.3 Downstream data usage modes 

For each downstream task, we compare four data usage modes introduced in Section D.1, which are used to explore how synthetic and local data is combined in the downstream tasks. Mode 1 (M1) uses only synthetic data. Mode 2 (M2) uses only the client’s local data (and therefore does not use federated learning). Mode 3 (M3) pools local observations with a subset of the synthetic observations directly in a mixed-data objective. Mode 4 (M4) combines local and synthetic observations through a loss-reweighted objective, where the synthetic component receives a tunable weight. 

6.2 Simulation experiments 

6.2.1 Simulation design 

The simulation study uses a mixed-type tabular data-generating mechanism with six covariates, 

(XB , X G1 , X G2 , X O, X L, X G3 ), and two task-specific responses, Ylin for linear regression and Ylog 

for logistic regression. The variable XB is binary, XO is ordinal, and the remaining covariates are continuous. The dependence structure among the covariates and the model settings are shown in Section D.2. Operationally, the generator first produces a shared synthetic covariate table according to the chain order (XB , X G1 , X G2 , X O, X L, X G3 ). The two task-specific responses, Ylin and Ylog , are then generated on top of this same covariate table. This design ensures that the linear and logistic downstream comparisons differ only in the response-generating mechanism, while sharing the same generated covariate distribution. This shared-covariate design removes sampling variation in the covariate distribution as a confounder when comparing the regression and classification tasks. More details are described in Table S1. 

6.2.2 Baseline comparisons 

We conduct three sets of experiments in this section. First, we compare FITS-G with two baselines, HeteroFedSyn and FedTabDiff, across all modes and clients. We report the parameter estimation errors of linear and logistic regression, as well as the classification accuracy of FT-Transformer. Second, we compare the Gaussian-kernel and KNN-kernel schemes, denoted by FITS-G and FITS-K, respectively. Third, we evaluate FITS-G under the four modes and their respective parameter settings to identify effective ways to use FITS-generated synthetic data. All results are reported as the mean (standard deviation) over 50 repeats, with lower errors and higher accuracy indicating better performance. Unless stated otherwise, we always use all available synthetic data and each client’s local data for model training in M3. M4 also uses all local and synthetic data but employs a weighted loss function, in which λ = 0 .5 specifies the weight assigned to each synthetic observation. For each mode and client, the best result among the compared methods is shown in bold. Overall, FITS-generated synthetic data yields better performance than the baselines. Clients with smaller local datasets benefit more from synthetic data, which can even serve as an effective substitute for their local data in downstream model training. For example, in Table 2, training solely on FITS-G synthetic data (M1) begins to outperform training solely on local data (M2) at Client 6 (6%), with parameter estimation errors of 0.1584 and 0.1751 , respectively. Similarly, in Table 3, this advantage begins at Client 3 (9%). Table 4 also shows that FITS-G improves FT-Transformer classification accuracy across all clients. Regarding data usage, combining synthetic and local data through M3 or M4 is more effective than using synthetic data alone through M1. Across these tables, M3 and M4 generally outperform M1; for FT-Transformer classification in Table 4, they also outperform M2. Next, we compare FITS-G and FITS-K in Tables S3, S4, and S5. The performance differences between the Gaussian-kernel and KNN-kernel schemes are negligible across the downstream tasks. These findings support the effectiveness of the proposed KNN-kernel scheme for FITS-based synthetic data generation. The preceding experiments show that data usage modes affect downstream performance differently. In the third set of experiments, we therefore vary the parameters of M3 and M4 for FITS-G to investigate how synthetic data can be used more effectively. In Tables S6, S7, and S8, the best result across all modes and parameter settings is shown in bold for each client. These results suggest that M4 is an effective way to exploit synthetic data, provided that the synthetic-observation loss weight 26 λ is chosen appropriately. 

6.2.3 Distributional fidelity 

Synthetic data needs to exhibit distributional fidelity. In this section, we use a distributional metric called Energy Distance (ED, Székely and Rizzo [2013]) to assess the fidelity of this synthetic data. Compared with the commonly used KL divergence, ED can be estimated directly from samples without requiring density estimation and avoids the infinite divergence that may occur due to mismatched supports between original and synthetic distributions. 

DE ( ˆP , ˆQ) =

where ˆP is the empirical distribution of the sample {xi}ni=1 drawn from P ; similarly, ˆQ is the empirical distribution of the sample {yi}mi=1 drawn from Q. We evaluate the distributional distance between the synthetic data and the full data including the training samples and test samples. We use the full data as the reference because it includes the training data used for synthesis and, owing to its larger sample size, is expected to provide a more reliable approximation to the underlying data distribution. This choice allows us to assess synthetic-data fidelity against a reference that incorporates the training observations while more broadly representing the underlying distribution. In the experiment, continuous and ordered variables are standardized using the means and standard deviations computed from the real reference data. Unordered categorical variables are one-hot encoded and each one-hot block is divided by √2, giving unit Euclidean distance between distinct categories. In Table 5, we compute three Energy Distances to assess the fidelity of the covariate distribution, the joint distribution of the covariates and the regression response, and the joint distribution of the covariates and the classification response. FITS-G gives the lowest ED values among all synthetic data generators, providing the best distributional fidelity for the full-dimensional tabular data. 6.3 Real-data experiments 

6.3.1 Datasets and preprocessing 

We next evaluate the method on two real mixed-type tabular data sets. The first is the UCI Bike Sharing data set, used for a linear regression task with response variable cnt . After preprocessing, the dataset contains 17 ,379 observations, of which 13 ,903 are used for training and 3,476 for testing. As in the simulation study, the training set is randomly split across ten clients according to the fixed propor-tion vector (p1, ..., p 10 ), yielding client sizes of {4171 , 2781 , 1391 , 1251 , 1112 , 973 , 834 , 695 , 556 , 139 }.The preprocessed predictors consist of four continuous variables and eight discrete variables. The response is standardized before fitting the generator and downstream linear models. The second real-data experiment uses the Adult Income dataset for binary classification. After preprocessing, the dataset contains 45 ,222 observations, with 36 ,177 used for training and 9,045 for testing. The training dataset is randomly split according to the fixed proportion vector (p1, ..., p 10 ), yielding client sizes of {10853 , 7235 , 3618 , 3256 , 2894 , 2532 , 2171 , 1809 , 1447 , 362 }. The predictors include five continuous variables and nine discrete variables. The binary income response is encoded as 0/1. For both data sets, missing or invalid entries are removed during preprocessing. Continuous predictors are standardized using complete-data means and standard deviations. Appendix D.3 provides more detailed information about the datasets and the chain order in FITS. Unlike the simulation study, the true downstream parameters are unavailable in the real-data experiments. Therefore, parameter error is computed relative to the full-data reference estimator fitted on the complete preprocessed training and test data. Prediction metrics are computed on the test set. 

6.3.2 Bike dataset 

We evaluate regression performance on the Bike dataset. As in the simulation study, this evaluation comprises three sets of experiments and an assessment of distributional fidelity. Tables 6, 7, and 8 present the first set of experiments comparing the synthetic data generation methods, with λ fixed at 0.1 for M4. The results show that FITS achieves the best performance across the regression tasks and models considered on this dataset. The second set of experiments compares FITS-G and FITS-K, as reported in Tables S9, S10, and S11. The two schemes yield broadly comparable performance on the regression tasks. The third set of experiments, presented in Tables S12, S13, and S14, further suggests that M4, with an appropriately chosen λ, provides an effective way to combine synthetic data with real local data. In particular, M4 with λ = 1 is equivalent to M3 with r = ∞. Regarding distributional fidelity, the Energy Distance results in Table 9 indicate that FITS more faithfully preserves the joint distribution of the Bike data. 28 Table 6: Bike: linear-regression parameter estimation error. 29 6.3.3 Adult dataset 

We evaluate classification performance on the Adult dataset using the same experimental structure. Tables 10, 11, and 12 compare the synthetic data generation methods, with λ fixed at 0.05 for M4. The comparisons between FITS-G and FITS-K are presented in Tables S15, S16, and S17. Finally, Tables S18, S19, and S20 report the performance of FITS-G across the four modes and their respective parameter settings. In contrast to the findings on the other datasets, the results here show that HeteroFedSyn achieves lower parameter estimation errors than FITS for logistic regression (Table 10). However, the two methods yield comparable test classification accuracy (Table 11), while FITS achieves the best overall performance with the more expressive FT-Transformer model (Table 12). One possible explanation is that two-way marginals capture much of the distributional structure relevant to logistic regression on this dataset. Preserving these pairwise relationships may therefore allow HeteroFedSyn to achieve better logistic-regression parameter estimates than FITS. In contrast, FT-Transformer can exploit richer, higher-order dependencies, making the preservation of more distributional information beyond two-way marginals more important. HeteroFedSyn may preserve these dependencies less effectively, which could explain its weaker performance relative to FITS with FT-Transformer. The Energy Distance results in Table 13 are consistent with this interpretation: although HeteroFedSyn also achieves relatively low distances, FITS exhibits the highest overall distributional fidelity. 7 Conclusion 

In this paper, we propose FITS, a federated nonparametric framework for generating synthetic mixed-type tabular data. The framework is built on three main components. First, we develop a two-step federated local linear procedure for conditional CDF estimation. The first communication step aggregates local weighted Hessian-type matrices and broadcasts the corresponding intercept coefficient vector, while the second step aggregates response-dependent local CDF contributions. Under fixed query points and weights, this construction recovers the pooled local linear estimator without centralizing raw observations. Second, we introduce a continualization scheme for discrete variables, together with an explicit inverse mapping to the original discrete domain. This construction lifts mixed mass-density structures to a piecewise continuous representation, allowing continuous and discrete variables to be handled within a unified smoothing framework. Third, we develop client-designed kernels with fixed-bandwidth and KNN-based schemes, together with a penalty-adaptive kernel that adjusts penalties for mismatches in continualized discrete covariates to accommodate mixed-type data. These designs improve the practical stability of the estimator in federated settings with mixed covariate types. On the theoretical side, we characterize the proposed continualization at the level of distribution laws. The results show that the continualized representation preserves probability mass while inducing a piecewise continuous joint density and corresponding conditional CDFs on the lifted domain. For the federated conditional CDF estimator, we establish uniform convergence over both response and covariate directions under the continualized support structure and Gaussian-kernel smoothing. These results provide the theoretical guarantees underlying the FITS framework. As the final goal for FITS, we construct the synthetic-data generator based on the chain rule and inverse conditional-CDF sampling, and evaluate its utility through downstream learning tasks under several modes of combining local and synthetic data. The experiments highlight three factors governing the utility of synthetic data: client sample size, model capacity, and training mode. 31 For clients with limited data, training on synthetic samples alone can outperform training solely on local observations. The benefits also depend on the downstream model: expressive models such as FT-Transformer can exploit synthetic data effectively. Across the experiments, weighted augmentation through M4 provides an effective way to combine synthetic and local observations, with the weight λ playing a crucial role. Several questions remain for future work. One direction is to develop more principled weighting schemes for combining local and synthetic data in downstream learning. Another is to characterize the utility boundary of federated synthetic data, including when synthetic samples are beneficial, redundant, or potentially harmful for a given client. It is also of interest to study stronger privacy guarantees, communication-efficient grid construction, and ordering strategies for the chain-rule generator. These extensions would further clarify the statistical and practical scope of federated synthetic data.

The pseudo-true parameter β⋄ is deterministic and depends on the bandwidths (wz , w x); it converges to β⋆(z, x) as wz , w x → 0. This deterministic intermediate quantity gives the natural decomposition of the overall estimation error into a stochastic component and a smoothing-induced (oracle) bias:

C Other proofs in Section 5 

C.1 Proofs for the lifted joint and conditional laws 

This appendix contains the proofs of the three regularity results stated in Section 5.3: Theorem 5.2 (blockwise continuity and one-sided limits of the lifted joint density), Corollary 5.2 (the Cr extension), and Theorem 5.3 (the corresponding piecewise structure of the lifted conditional distribution function). The three results form a logical chain in which each proof relies on the preceding one: C0-regularity provides the geometric backbone, Cr-regularity follows by applying the C0 argument coordinatewise to each partial derivative, and the conditional-distribution structure is then derived by combining Cr-regularity with the probability law identity P(Z ≤ z | X(c), Y ∈ I(υ)) = P(Z ≤ z | X(c), X(d) = υ).Throughout this appendix we adopt the notation introduced in Sections 4.1 and 4.2: the discrete support Υ = Q ˜dj=1 Υj with blocks {I(υ)}υ∈Υ , the continualized covariate eX = ( X(c), Y) on 

eX = Xc × Y , the interior region eX ◦ and the covariate jumping set eX •.47 C.2 Proof of Theorem 5.2

D Experimental settings 

D.1 Data usage modes for downstream learning 

A federated synthetic dataset is most useful when it can be combined with the original local data to support downstream learning tasks at each client. This subsection formalizes four data usage modes for downstream training that differ in how synthetic and local data is weighted in the empirical loss. These four modes are not algorithmic variants of the synthetic data generator itself but four well-defined ways of using the generated data in downstream learning; they constitute the comparison framework that Section 6 evaluates empirically. Throughout this subsection, let B = { ˆxb}Bb=1 denote the synthetic dataset generated by Algo-rithm 2 and shared across all clients, and let H(k) denote client k’s local dataset. We consider a generic downstream loss ℓ indexed by a parameter θ (for example, a squared-error loss for linear regression or a negative log-likelihood loss for logistic regression), and define the four data usage modes as follows. 

M1: synthetic-only learning. Client k fits its downstream model entirely on the shared synthetic dataset: 

bθ(k)syn = arg min 

> θ

X

> ˆxb∈B

ℓ(θ; ˆ xb). (61) This mode is the natural baseline for clients with no or extremely limited local data. It captures all the information that the federated framework has aggregated about the joint law, but it does not exploit any client-specific signal. 

M2: local-only learning. Client k fits the downstream model on its local data alone: 

bθ(k)loc = arg min 

> θ

X

> hi∈H (k)

ℓ(θ; hi). (62) 62 This is the standard non-federated baseline: no benefit is drawn from the synthetic data, and each client’s estimator is governed by its local sample size mk.

M3: mixed-data learning. Client k pools a subset of the synthetic observations Bpart and 

|B part | = r|H (k)|, r ∈ [0 , ∞], whose size is controlled by r relative to the local sample size, with its local dataset to form a single training set with uniform weight per sample. 

bθ(k)mix = arg min 

> θ

 X

> hi∈H (k)

ℓ(θ; hi) + X

> ˆxb∈B part

ℓ(θ; ˆ xb)



. (63) Each synthetic and local sample contributes equally to the empirical loss, but the number of synthetic observations is controlled. Note that the parameter r = ∞ means that all synthetic data is used. 

M4: loss-reweighted synthetic-local learning. Client k combines synthetic and local data with a tunable weight λ ∈ [0 , +∞) on the synthetic loss: 

bθ(k)rew (λ) = arg min 

> θ

 X

> hi∈H (k)

ℓ(θ; hi) + λX

> ˆxb∈B

ℓ(θ; ˆ xb)



. (64) The weight λ controls the relative influence of the two data sources. The special case λ = 0 recovers Mode 2, whereas λ = 1 recovers Mode 3 with the full synthetic dataset. The four modes differ in how synthetic data replaces or complements local data. Mode 1 completely replaces local data with synthetic data; Mode 2 ignores the synthetic data entirely; and Mode 3 controls the relative contribution of synthetic data through the number of synthetic observations selected by r relative to mk. Mode 4 controls the trade-off explicitly at the loss-function level through λ.

D.2 Simulation data 

The simulated covariates are generated as follows. First, 

XB,i ∼ Bernoulli( π), π = 0 .45 .

Conditional on XB,i , the pair (XG1,i , X G2,i ) is sampled from a bivariate Gaussian distribution: 

(XG1,i , X G2,i )⊤ | XB,i = 0 ∼ N

Thus XB drives the distribution of (XG1 , X G2 ), the ordinal variable XO depends on (XB , X G1 , X G2 ),and XL depends on (XB , X G1 , X G2 , X O), while XG3 is generated independently. E Baseline methods 

We compare FITS with HeteroFedSyn [Li et al., 2026] and FedTabDiff [Sattarov et al., 2024]. Both baselines generate a shared synthetic tabular dataset B = { ˆxb}Bb=1 from the same client datasets {H (k)}Kk=1 , where mk = |H (k)| and M = PKk=1 mk. FT-Transformer provides an additional downstream evaluation model. The descriptions below summarize the baseline implementations used in our experiments. 

E.1 HeteroFedSyn 

We use the adaptive marginal-selection variant, AdaFedPrivSyn. Continuous columns are discretized into at most 100 equal-width bins, while discrete columns retain their observed categories. For a table with q columns, client k computes one-way count vectors c(k) 

> a

and two-way count vectors 

c(k) 

> ab

. Each attribute pair uses a shared Gaussian projection matrix Πab ∈ Rcacb×10 , where ca is the 65 encoded domain size of column a. The server aggregates locally perturbed statistics as 

ˆpa = 1

M

> K

X

> k=1

+ ξ(k)

> a

, ˆsab = 1

M

> K

 Π⊤

c(k) 

> ab

> ab

Here ξ(k) 

> a

and ζ(k) 

> ab

are Gaussian noise vectors. Dependency scores compare ˆsab with the projected product Π⊤

> ab

( ˆ pa ⊗ ˆpb), with a correction for the aggregated noise variance. A pair is selected when its dependency score exceeds its expected release-noise error. We select at most ⌊ q

> 2

/3⌋ pairs. After each selection, marginal consistency adjustment and 30 iterations of the gradual update method (GUM, Zhang et al. [2021]) produce a synthetic tabular dataset; projected residuals update the scores of remaining pairs involving covered attributes. Previously released one-way marginals are reused. Continuous values are recovered as bin midpoints, and discrete values are mapped back to their original categories. We set ϵ = 10 8 and δ = 10 −5. Writing L = log(1 /δ ), the implementation uses the zCDP budget 

ρ =  √L + ϵ − √L2, (ρ1, ρ 2, ρ 3) = (0 .1ρ, 0.1ρ, 0.8ρ),

for one-way statistics, projected two-way statistics, and selected two-way marginals, respectively. This deliberately weak privacy setting emphasizes synthesis utility rather than a privacy–utility trade-off. 

E.2 FedTabDiff 

FedTabDiff learns a denoising diffusion model on an encoded mixed-type tabular dataset. Continuous columns are transformed to approximately standard normal marginals using a training-data quantile transform with at most 1,000 quantiles. Each discrete column uses a two-dimensional frozen embedding; generated embeddings are decoded to the nearest category embedding within that column. The encoded record z0 is perturbed according to 

zt = √¯αtz0 + √1 − ¯αtε, ε ∼ N (0, I ), ¯αt =

> t

Y

> u=0

(1 − βu).

Clients minimize the noise-prediction loss E∥ε − εψ(zt, t )∥22. All clients participate in every commu-nication round, and the server uses sample-size-weighted FedAvg: 

ψ(s+1) =

> K

X

> k=1

mk

M ψ(s+1)  

> k

,

where ψ(s+1)  

> k

is the k-th client’s model at the (s + 1) -th iteration and ψ(s+1) is the corresponding central model. The noise predictor uses four hidden layers of width 1,024 . We use 500 diffusion steps with a linear noise schedule from 2 × 10 −4 to 0.04 , and 1,000 FedAvg rounds with Adam (learning rate 

10 −4), batch size 512 , and at most 20 local updates per client per round. Covariates and responses are synthesized jointly. 66 E.3 FT-Transformer for downstream evaluation 

We use FT-Transformer [Gorishniy et al., 2021] to evaluate nonlinear regression and classification on the generated data. For each M1–M4 fit, only covariates enter the model. Continuous features are standardized using the weighted mean and standard deviation of that fit’s training data, while nominal and ordinal predictors are mapped to categorical indices. Each standardized continuous feature x(c)

j receives an affine token, and each discrete feature x(d)

j

receives a learned category embedding: 

tj =

(

x(c)

j aj + bj , x(c)

j continuous ,Ej [x(d)

j ] + bj , x(d)

j discrete , tj ∈ R32 ,

where aj , bj ∈ R32 are learnable parameter vectors and Ej is a learnable embedding matrix for discrete feature j.The feature tokens and a learned [CLS] token have dimension 32 and pass through two Transformer blocks. Each block contains residual self-attention with four heads and a feed-forward network of width 64 with ReGLU activation. Attention, feed-forward, and residual dropout are all set to zero. The final [CLS] representation passes through LayerNorm, ReLU, and a linear head to produce a scalar output fθ(x), used for regression or as a binary-classification logit. For a training table T = {(xi, y i, w i)}ni=1 specified by the data usage mode, the objective is 

L(θ) = 

Pni=1 wiℓ(θ; xi, y i)

Pni=1 wi

, ℓ(θ; x, y ) = 

(

(fθ(x) − y)2, regression ,

log(1 + efθ (x)) − yf θ(x), classification .

Regression responses are additionally standardized using training weights, and predictions are mapped back to the evaluation scale. Binary responses retain their 0/1 encoding; the sigmoid of the model output gives the class probability, with threshold 0.5.Each model uses 500 AdamW updates with learning rate 10 −3, batch size 256 , and weight decay 

10 −5. Minibatches are sampled with replacement with probabilities wi/ P

j wj . Training settings and seed rules are identical across methods. We report regression test MSE on the original response scale and classification test accuracy.
